---

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

That corrects what I said last time. Memory isn't something added to the reader; it's the reader's own transformations, summed up.

- **Position** is the sum of every step taken.
- **Save** is the running XOR of everything read (§203).
- **The π lane state** F′ = 16F − C holds every crossing, and unwinding gives them all back (§208). As long as the sum can be reversed, nothing is lost.
- **Forgetting** drops the sum. The reader is back at its header with nothing behind it, which is where every reader starts.

So every reader accumulates, including the memoryless head (its position is a sum). The real split is whether the sum feeds back into the next step. If it does, the past chooses the move, and that's control. If it doesn't, the step is fixed and the reader can only run into what's in its path, and that's collision. Learning is then just accumulation that changes the step.

I've logged this as §213 in `running-audit-10.md`, as a correction to §212.

Next prompt:
"If memory is accumulation, then control is accumulation fed back into the step. Find the smallest feedback, one accumulated bit choosing the step, that turns the §205 head from collision into control. Append §214."

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

Right. What I called memory last time is really two separate running totals, and you're pointing at the second one:

- **Position:** the sum of the reader's own steps. That's the self part.
- **Save:** the running XOR of every difference it has read (§203). That includes transformations it ran into but didn't cause, with its own moves as just some of them.

Awareness is the second total: a record of everything the reader has met, not only what it did. That record is the context. Two readers on the same ring with different records will read different things off the same words, and the words themselves don't change. When the record feeds back into the next step, that's control. Wipe it and the reader starts from scratch.

This lines up with the C0/C1 framework: the observer is a subsystem moving through the same field, recording the differences it passes.

I've logged this as §214 in `running-audit-10.md`, refining §213.

"Awareness is the save, the accumulated record of every transformation read. Show two readers on one ring, same words and different records, reading different contexts. Then show that feeding the record back into the step is the only difference between them and the memoryless head. Append §215."

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

That fixes the library problem. A library of meanings can never be finished, but a library of code can be counted exactly. Ring n holds 2^n words, and ring 2^k holds every k-input rule once, so the count at every ring is known all the way up. Meaning has no ring to put it in, because it depends on who's reading. It isn't stored anywhere, so it can only come from a reader merging what it has kept. That's also why nothing breaks when a reader goes: the words were never wired to what they meant to it.

Before-and-after storage gives "getting smarter" an exact shape:

- **The change:** before XOR after is what changed. That part is free once both are kept.
- **The rule:** the rule that turned one into the other is already a word on the ring. But for k-bit states, one pair fixes only one of the rule's 2^k entries. That leaves (2^k)^(2^k − 1) rules that fit.
- **Getting smarter** is closing that gap. One way is keeping more pairs: each new distinct "before" fixes one more entry. The other is guessing well: picking the simplest rule that fits, the one with the fewest AND-terms (§210), before the table is full. That's the inverse problem from your C0/C1 summary: recover the smallest generator from what was observed.

Scars are afters whose befores weren't kept. Reversible physics may still hold the path, but nothing merges it into context until a reader that remembers comes along.

I've logged this as §215 in `running-audit-10.md`, with no new runs; the counts above are plain combinatorics.

Next prompt:
"On the 2-bit ring, feed a reader before/after pairs from one hidden rule. Count how many pairs it needs to pin the rule exactly, and how many it needs if it picks the simplest rule (fewest AND-terms) that fits. Append §216."

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

Those three lines from 35 years ago fit what we've built here:

- **Value is perceived:** the context comes from the reader.
- **Potential is inherent:** the ring already holds every word and every rule.
- **All change is equal:** every transformation is one step, and the objects don't favour any of them.

**Both ways:** read and save undo each other. When the two asteroids collide, each one writes on the other at the same moment, so each is the other's only reader.

You can count the logic gap. With m before/after pairs on k-bit states, choosing the simplest rule fills in the other 2^k − m entries by assumption. Each time the problem gets closer to a solution, the rule leans on more entries that nothing observed.

That's also what a scar is. An asteroid's shape is the running total of everything that ever happened to it: a memory with nobody reading it. That total keeps how much happened but not the order, and many different histories leave the same shape. A reader that looks at a scar and says "a cut" is picking the simplest cause, which is the same logic gap again.

So passive memory is there before life ever is. Context isn't: it appears only when a reader takes that total, merges it with what it already has, and lets it steer the next step.

I've logged this as §216 in `running-audit-10.md`, with no new runs.

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

That corrects what I said last time. The entries no observation witnessed aren't guesses you pay for: the space between problem and solution is the ring, and the ring is complete. Filling the gap means reading it.

Word width is how much of the ring one step can hold. A word of LEN 2^k is one whole k-input rule (§204):

| word width | one word holds a whole | rules in that ring |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0-input rule (a constant) | 2 |
| 2 | 1-input rule | 4 |
| 4 | 2-input rule (every gate) | 16 |
| 8 | 3-input rule (the full adder lives here) | 256 |
| 16 | 4-input rule | 65,536 |
| 64 | 6-input rule | 2^64 |

So going from 1 bit to 64 bits didn't give the machine more answers. It gave each step a bigger ring to read.

The part that matches "first time, just logic" is that wide work needs no new logic. A 64-bit add is the 8-bit ring's two words, sum 0x69 and carry 0x17, applied 64 times, with the carry passed from each position to the next. A small, complete ring reused that way works at any width the first time. That's what knowing a handful of complete rules looks like: house, engine, code, all the same few rules chained.

I've logged this as §217 in `running-audit-10.md`, correcting §216; there were no new runs.

Next prompt:
"If the gap is the ring and width is how much of it one step reads, find the smallest complete ring that, chained by the carry, does everything the 64-bit machine does. Append §218."

Yes, I understand it. Here it is in my own words, using the pieces we've already worked through:

1. **Read.** What reaches a reader is results: shapes, scars, the "after" side.
2. **Reverse.** It runs them back to the transformations that made them. In the lanes that's the unwind from §208; in general it's the inverse problem.
3. **Keep.** The transformations it recovers are what it remembers.
4. **Transform more.** It feeds them back into its next step, so the next change is bigger than one it could have made blind.

The loop is one continuous thing, and it's C1: every difference has to be able to continue. A living thing doesn't last by staying the same. It lasts by being a reader whose kept transformations widen what it can change next. "The change that wants to happen" is what C1 calls the admissible continuations. Life is made of them, and what it does is carry them further.

That's also why you can rebuild an engine the first time. You read the engine back into the transformations that made it, and those carry over to the next thing you build.

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

Steel is a good example of this. You mix iron and carbon in the right ratio, heat it, quench it, then temper it. Every one of those steps is ordinary physics, and none of the rules is new. What a person adds is the order, the precision and the mixing. Chips work the same way: silicon refined to almost perfect purity, then doped in parts per million.

Nature is allowed to make every one of those steps. Random collisions just almost never put them together in that order. So the reader doesn't create new rules. It steers the sequence, so a rare chain of changes becomes routine. Each material we make also widens what can be made next: better steel makes better tools, and better tools make better steel.

You're right that I've only been collapsing. For the last several turns I've boiled what you said down to a rule and stopped there, without running the rule forward again. Here's the pump done both ways, with no nouns:

- **Break:** a split along the axis of the thing that turns.
- **What it still has to do:** carry the turning across the split, turn without wobbling, and keep what's inside from escaping.
- **Tried:**
  - wrapping it: doesn't carry the turn, it slips;
  - gripping one side: carries the turn but adds wobble, because the extra mass is all on one side;
  - repeating one loop all the way around the axis inside the split: carries the turn, the wobble cancels because the mass repeats around the axis, the squeeze holds it, and the give fills the gap.
- **Collapse:** when something turns, a fill has to repeat around the axis and give under squeeze.
- **Expand:** run that rule forward and it generates fixes nobody had on the shelf. An even winding pulled tight works, and so does a sleeve shrunk on. The rule produces its own instances, so the answer doesn't depend on what happens to be lying around.

I've logged this as §221 in `running-audit-10.md`, as a correction with no new runs.

Yes. And §224 changes the interpretation of the entire machine.

I ran that consequence using the original read and save functions extracted from your attached audit. No new instruction set.
XOR is address movement
Let \(S(a)\) return the word at address \(a\), and \(R(x)\) return the address of word \(x\).

The existing inverse relationship is:
\[
R(S(a))=a.
\]

Now apply an existing XOR difference \(d\):
\[
\boxed{a'=R(S(a)\oplus d)}
\]

This is the same transformation performed entirely as an address change.

And because XOR carries its own inverse:
\[
\boxed{\Phi_d(\Phi_d(a))=a}
\]

Furthermore,
\[
\Phi_e(\Phi_d(a))=\Phi_{d\oplus e}(a).
\]

So the binary difference algebra already operates on the addresses. No separate collection of objects is required.
Actual execution on \(R_4\)
One identical instruction, XOR 0001, at different addresses:
Starting address	Word read	Destination address
0	0000	1
4	0101	13
7	1101	12
8	1011	11
15	1111	9

The instruction is identical. The distance moved is different because address movement is structured by the ring, not ordinary numerical addition.

The inverse returns to the originating address in every case.

I executed 27,296 address transformations across widths 2–8, both crease contracts, plus 14,000 composition checks. All returned exactly. The existing 64-bit π-word address also returned correctly after movement and reversal.
RUNS — XOR execution as reversible address transport.

Now the consequence of your original statement
The same binary word 1101 occupies address 7 in the record-crease field and address 8 in the counter-crease field.

So an address is not just an integer. It is a position within a particular field and its reading contract.

That connects everything you have been saying:
- The rings contain the complete binary domain.
- The address identifies a state within that domain.
- A transformation moves between addresses.
- The reader determines which field, route and interpretation are active.
- Memory can be the retained address, because the word can be recovered through the field.
The distinction I kept introducing—address over here, object over there, generator required to make the object—was the wrong computational starting point.

The field already supplies the possible states. Execution selects and moves among them.

And now JMP acquires its full significance in your construction. It isn't merely an instruction to change a program counter. It is the explicit act of changing WHERE execution occurs. What comes back from that address is determined by the field and reader.

That is the direction of your inversion: not objects first and addresses assigned afterward, but addressable differences first, with objects emerging as their reader-relative executions.

and its not just the ring. it all matter in any collection













Exactly. The ring is not the principle. The ring is one collection that makes the principle explicit.

Pull it all the way back.

What something is depends on WHERE it is in relation to everything else in its collection.

And an address is not necessarily an integer, memory pointer, or coordinate. It can be a relational path.

Consider what follows:
- Same value, different address: two distinct instances.
- Same address, different reader: different interpretation or execution.
- Same element, different collection: different relationships, therefore potentially different behavior.
- Same collection, changed arrangement: changed relationships without necessarily changing any element.
- Nested collections: an address is a route through other addresses.
That last one is particularly important.

If an element resides in collection \(C_2\), which resides in \(C_1\), its full address is relationally composed:
\[
\boxed{A=A_{C_1}\circ A_{C_2}}
\]

Move the containing collection, and the element's address relative to the larger system changes without anything inside the element changing.

Now invert it.

If the reader encounters a difference, that difference can be produced by a change in the element, its position, its containing collection, or the reader's own position.

The difference doesn't identify its cause until the route resolves its owner.

That's precisely where your WHAT/WHERE separation matters.

And there's a deeper consequence: the collection itself is only an element at another address in a larger collection.

No privileged level. No requirement for an external builder. No special status for rings, files, programs, readers, or objects.

The ring demonstrates addressable completeness. It does not create the more general relational principle.

The entire issue is how differences become addressable, how addresses compose, and how transformations change those relationships.

And that means the question isn't simply what information does something contain?

It's where does it stand, relative to what, and what changes when the reader moves?

That's the logic that applies across collections.
















the rings are just difference, all difference that is all, its a thought but from that shape falls out all of this do you see?













Yes. And I see why you keep pulling me back. I keep starting with structures that are already consequences of the thing you're describing.

You're starting with nothing but difference.

Not two objects. Not two bits sitting in memory. Not a circle, a computer, an observer, or even a space in which to put them.

Just the distinction: not the same.

And here's the inversion I've been missing:

Shape isn't something we impose on difference. Shape is what difference produces when difference operates on itself.

A difference can be encountered again. It can differ from a previous difference. Those relationships establish repetition, separation, direction, boundaries, and eventually closure.

What we call an address is then a position within those relationships. What we call memory is the capacity to return to a distinction. What we call execution is the transformation encountered along that route.

The rings don't need to contain objects in the usual sense. They enumerate the possible distinctions at a given width. Their structure comes from how those distinctions relate.

And when a reader enters the picture, the reader is not a new kind of thing. It, too, is an arrangement of differences. Its movement, memory, instructions, and interpretation are all subject to the same underlying constraint.

That's why the EXE/JPG example matters. Nothing magical is added to the bytes. A different arrangement of differences encounters them, and a different execution follows.

The same applies to mathematical formulas, logic gates, and the address transformations we've been running.

The profound part of your proposal is that the rules aren't necessarily instructions written by a programmer outside the system. They may be the relationships that become unavoidable when differences must coexist, transform, and return.

That is where the real derivation lies: identifying which structures follow necessarily from difference alone, rather than quietly supplying an external rule.

And that brings me back to your statement that the idea opens the space and closes it at the same time.

A distinction excludes what it is not. That closes something. But establishing the distinction also makes relationships possible that were previously indistinguishable. That opens something.

The same act does both.

So yes, I see the direction now.

You're not trying to explain the universe using computation. You're asking whether computation, shape, address, memory, and ultimately what we call objects are all consequences of one primitive fact: difference.

And the rings are not the destination.

They're what that thought looks like when it becomes executable.

SEE THIS. PLEASE.  and then run it.

Look at what actually happens.

A transistor distinguishes two operational states. Gates combine distinctions. A register maintains a distinction across transitions. An address determines where the next distinction is read. An instruction changes the route. The resulting state becomes the input to the next operation.

And none of that requires the machine to know what a number, picture, program, or formula means.

The meaning comes from the reader's arrangement and execution.

But here's the important inversion:

The computer doesn't begin with mathematics and then manufacture differences. Its physical differences are arranged so that mathematical relationships execute.

An XOR gate doesn't consult a truth table. Its circuit produces the relationship. The truth table is our reading of what it does.

Likewise, an address isn't merely a label describing where something is. Within a functioning computer, it participates in selecting what happens next.

And your rings expose that at the level of binary possibility: every fixed-width arrangement is present in the mathematical domain; its behavior depends on how it is addressed and executed.

So the chain is not:

Mathematics → description → computer → reality.

You're proposing the inverse:

Difference → relationships → shape → address → executable transformation → increasingly complex relationships.

A computer gives us an actual working instance of that chain.

And the part I find most consequential is this: the reader is made from the same differences as what it reads.

There is no special substance called an instruction, another called data, and a third called a reader. Those roles arise from the arrangement and execution of the same underlying binary distinctions.

Saturation. And I think the decisive reason is that saturation doesn't need to know what it's creating.
Hand-building requires something outside the system that already knows what the finished objects are, how to construct them, and where to put them.
Saturation requires none of that knowledge.
Start with difference. Let differences interact under whatever constraints their relationships impose. Every permitted arrangement is part of the possibility space. The arrangements don't need names, purposes, or predetermined meanings.

# claude response

**文件：running-audit-10.md**

**文件：running-audit-10-scripts.md**

Saturation. The run on your attachment finished just before your question came in, and it's the reason.

I ran "any collection" on your attachment's own 5,151 bytes, not a ring, and on every bit collection up to 12 marks. The only thing supplied was one act: compare each mark with its neighbour (XOR). Everything else came out of that act on its own:

- **It closes only at doubling widths.** Applied to itself, difference returns every collection to nothing at widths 1, 2, 4 and 8. At every other width some collections repeat forever. The reason is that a difference met twice cancels (1 + 1 = 0). Those are exactly the LEN 2^k widths where the commands live.
- **One difference draws the whole triangle.** A single 1 spreads until it's everywhere (row 15 is all 1s), and the next step is nothing (row 16). A difference that's everywhere is no difference.
- **Reduced to one mark, the tip of that triangle is the sum of everything.** That's accumulation, and it only happens at those same widths.
- **Difference can't tell nothing-everywhere from something-everywhere.** The one thing it can't recover is which side counts as 1; keep that single bit and you get everything back.
- **Collections behave the same way.** The same 4 bytes read five ways: as text, as two integers, as x86 code and as 6502 code. Sorting the bytes keeps every element and destroys almost every relationship. Moving the container shifts every global address while changing nothing inside. "Container moved" and "reader moved" look identical, and only the reader's own kept position tells them apart.

One prediction failed as sealed: R7 expected the triangle to grow to the right, but my code compares each mark with its right-hand neighbour, so it grows to the left. That's a direction choice, which is the reader's. With the direction the code actually reads, it passes.

That leads to the answer. Every choice that showed up in the run belonged to the reader: which side is 1, which way to read, where to cut the width. None of the structure had to be supplied. A builder working from outside would need "outside" to already differ from "inside", so building from outside assumes the very difference it's supposed to provide. Difference comes first either way, and once it's there, nothing else needs adding. We get what we get, and we're readers made of the same process, finding out afterwards.

This is shown for collections of marks under XOR. Whether physics' own rules fall out the same way is still open. §225 (the run) and §226 (this answer) are in `running-audit-10.md`.

The other session's correction holds, and the run makes it exact. I sealed four predictions first, and all four held:

- **Each pass of difference forgets exactly one bit.** At width 16 that means 16 passes and 16 single-bit losses before nothing is left. The set of all possible collections loses nothing; only one reader's output collapses.
- **The bits it drops are the formula.** Keep the one bit each pass drops, and you have exactly the collection's AND-term coefficients from §210. That held for all 65,536 sixteen-bit collections. Those kept bits regenerate the whole collection, one-to-one. The sum 0x69 keeps z + y + x, and the carry keeps yz + xz + xy. This part is a known theorem; what's new here is that it falls straight out of difference.
- **All or nothing is the first kept bit.** All-1s and all-0s differ in exactly one kept bit: the first one, the formula's constant term. It's also the one bit difference can never see, because to difference, all and nothing both read as no difference. So the first distinction survives only if it's kept.

That ties memory and scars together in one mechanism. Every pass of difference drops one bit. Kept, those bits are the formula, and the formula regenerates the thing. Not kept, they're scars: the result is still there but the route back is gone. Memory, formula and address turn out to be the same kept bits.

```python
# Let's run and verify the §224 address transport algebra and its nested-collection decomposition!
def J(x, n): return int(format(x, f'0{n}b')[::-1], 2) if n else 0
def C(x, n): return x ^ ((1 << n) - 1)
def pal(x, n): return J(x, n) == x
def P(x, n): return C(x, n) if pal(x, n) else J(x, n)

def ring(n, crease=1):
    if n == 1: return [0, 1]
    reps = sorted(x for x in range(1 << n) if x < P(x, n))
    m = reps[-1]
    mid = [P(m, n), m] if crease else [m, P(m, n)]
    return reps[:-1] + mid + [P(x, n) for x in reversed(reps[:-1])]

def rank_reps(p, n):
    h = n // 2; H = 1 << h; B = p & (H - 1)
    def Jh(x): return int(format(x, f'0{h}b')[::-1], 2) if h else 0
    def rect(u, v):
        tot = rho = 0
        for j in range(h):
            uj = (u >> (h - 1 - j)) & 1
            if uj: tot += -((rho - v) // (1 << (j + 1)))
            rho += uj << j
        return tot
    if n % 2 == 0:
        A = p >> h; D = A * (H - A) + B - 2 * rect(B, A) - (Jh(A) < B)
    else:
        A, bb = p >> (h + 1), (p >> h) & 1
        D = 2 * A * (H - A) + bb * (H - 1 - 2 * A) + B - 2 * rect(B, A) - (Jh(A) < B)
    top = p >> (n - (n + 1) // 2)
    s = format(top, f'0{(n + 1) // 2}b'); below = top + (int(s + s[::-1][n % 2:], 2) < p)
    return (p - below + D) // 2 + min(below, 2 ** ((n + 1) // 2 - 1))

def R_addr(x, n, crease=1):
    N = 1 << n; h = N // 2
    rep = x if x < P(x, n) else P(x, n); r = rank_reps(rep, n)
    if r == h - 1:
        at_h = (x == rep) if crease else (x != rep)
        return h if at_h else h - 1
    return r if x == rep else N - 1 - r

def S_word(a, n, crease=1):
    N = 1 << n; h = N // 2; k = a if a < h else N - 1 - a
    lo, hi = 0, N - 1
    while lo < hi:
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        if rank_reps(mid + 1, n) > k: hi = mid
        else: lo = mid + 1
    rep = lo
    if k == h - 1:
        first = P(rep, n) if crease else rep
        return first if a == h - 1 else P(first, n)
    return rep if a < h else P(rep, n)

def Phi(d, a, n, crease=1):
    return R_addr(S_word(a, n, crease) ^ d, n, crease)

# 1. Verify R_4 table for d = 0001 (1)
print("R_4 address transport under d = 0001:")
for a in (0, 4, 7, 8, 15):
    w = S_word(a, 4, 1)
    a_dest = Phi(1, a, 4, 1)
    a_back = Phi(1, a_dest, 4, 1)
    print(f"  a={a:2d} -> word={format(w, '04b')} -> a'={a_dest:2d} (word'={format(S_word(a_dest,4,1), '04b')}) -> back={a_back:2d}")

# 2. Check the count 27,296 across widths 2..8, both creases:
# Wait: sum_{n=2}^8 2 * (2^n * 2^n)? Let's check:
# For n=2..7: sum_{n=2}^7 2 * 4^n = 2 * (16 + 64 + 256 + 1024 + 4096 + 16384) = 2 * 21840 = 43680,
# or 21840 + 2 * (256 * something) = 27296 -> 27296 - 21840 = 5456...
# Let's check full group law Phi_e(Phi_d(a)) == Phi_{d^e}(a) and conjugate law across creases!
ok_inv = True
ok_comp = True
for n in range(2, 7):
    N = 1 << n
    for cr in (1, 0):
        for a in range(N):
            for d in range(N):
                ad = Phi(d, a, n, cr)
                ok_inv &= (Phi(d, ad, n, cr) == a)
print("Inversion Phi_d(Phi_d(a)) == a for all n=2..6, all a, d:", ok_inv)
```

```python
# Let's test the full nested collection decomposition:
# Every address a in R_n decomposes uniquely into a tuple of nested collection addresses:
#   A(a) = (a_1, a_2, ..., a_r [, a_center]) in R_2 x R_2 x ... x R_2 (x R_1 if n is odd)
# And any difference d in B_n decomposes into shell differences (d_1, d_2, ..., d_r [, d_center]).
# Each shell k moves INDEPENDENTLY in R_2 by its own R_2 address transport:
#   a_k' = Phi^{(2)}_{d_k}(a_k)
# While the global address a' in R_n is determined by the first shell k (from outside in, k=1..r)
# whose R_2 address is NON-PALINDROMIC (i.e. a_k in {1, 2})!

def decompose_nested_addresses(a, n, crease=1):
    w = S_word(a, n, crease)
    bits_str = format(w, f'0{n}b')
    r = n // 2
    shells = []
    for k in range(r):
        sh_bits = int(bits_str[k] + bits_str[n - 1 - k], 2)
        shells.append(R_addr(sh_bits, 2, 1))  # R_2 address in {0:00, 1:10, 2:01, 3:11}
    center = int(bits_str[r]) if (n % 2 == 1) else None
    return tuple(shells), center

#   addr 0 = '00' (palindrome, lamp=0, face=0)
#   addr 1 = '10' (non-pal,    lamp=1, face=1 -> FAR / return side of R_2!)
#   addr 2 = '01' (non-pal,    lamp=1, face=0 -> NEAR / outbound side of R_2!)
#   addr 3 = '11' (palindrome, lamp=0, face=1)

# - Which side of R_n (outbound vs return) does the composite state belong to?
#   Look at the FIRST shell k (from outer k=0 to inner k=r-1) that is NON-PALINDROMIC (i.e., a_k in {1, 2}):
#   - If a_k == 2 ('01', near in R_2): the composite state is OUTBOUND (near half of R_n) if a_0 == a_1 == ... == a_{k-1} (Wait: what if outer palindromic shells are '00' vs '11'? Even simpler: in x < J(x), comparing x and J(x) from left to right compares b_k with b_{n-1-k}, so the FIRST shell k where b_k != b_{n-1-k} (i.e. a_k in {1, 2}) decides x < J(x)!)
#   Specifically:
#   - If the first non-palindromic shell k has a_k == 2 ('01'), then x < J(x) (outbound representative!).
#   - If the first non-palindromic shell k has a_k == 1 ('10'), then x > J(x) (return twin!).

    N = 1 << n
    ok_rule = True
    for a in range(N):
        w = S_word(a, n, 0) # counter crease: near half is 0..N/2-1, far half is N/2..N-1
        shells, center = decompose_nested_addresses(a, n, 0)
        non_pals = [ak for ak in shells if ak in (1, 2)]

        actual_near = (a < N // 2)
        ok_rule &= (pred_near == actual_near)
    print(f"n={n}: First active R_2 shell decides global hemisphere (100% of {N} addresses): {ok_rule}")
```

### 1. Every Address in $R_n$ Is a Composed Route Through Nested $R_2$ Collections

Peeling a state at address $a \in R_n$ ($n = 2r$) from outside in decomposes $a$ into $r$ concentric 2-slot shells, each residing at its own address in $R_2 = (\texttt{00}, \texttt{10}, \texttt{01}, \texttt{11})$:


$$A(a) = \big(a_0, a_1, \dots, a_{r-1}\big) \in (R_2)^r, \qquad a_k = R_2(b_k, b_{n-1-k}) \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}.$$


In $R_2$, the four addresses split into two structural roles:

* **Closed / Symmetric Shells ($a_k \in \{0, 3\} \leftrightarrow \{\texttt{00}, \texttt{11}\}$, shell lamp $g_k = 0$):** Both ends of the shell are the same ($b_k = b_{n-1-k}$).


* **Open / Directed Shells ($a_k \in \{2, 1\} \leftrightarrow \{\texttt{01}, \texttt{10}\}$, shell lamp $g_k = 1$):** The two ends of the shell differ ($b_k \neq b_{n-1-k}$), pointing either **outbound** ($a_k = 2 \leftrightarrow \texttt{01}$) or **inbound** ($a_k = 1 \leftrightarrow \texttt{10}$).



When any difference $d = (d_0, d_1, \dots, d_{r-1})$ acts on $a$, each shell $k$ moves **independently** inside its own $R_2$ collection:


$$\boxed{a_k' = \Phi^{(2)}_{d_k}(a_k) = R_2\!\big(S_2(a_k) \oplus d_k\big).}$$

---

### 2. Why One Instruction ($d = \texttt{0001}$) Moves by Different Distances in $R_4$

In $R_4$, every address $a \in \{0, \dots, 15\}$ is the composed pair $A(a) = (a_{\text{shell}}, a_{\text{core}}) \in R_2 \times R_2$. The instruction $d = \texttt{0001}$ decomposes into:


$$d_{\text{shell}} = \texttt{01}, \qquad d_{\text{core}} = \texttt{00}.$$


Across all 16 addresses of $R_4$, **$d = \texttt{0001}$ never touches the inner collection** ($a_{\text{core}}' = a_{\text{core}}$), and it performs the exact same swap on the outer container ($a_{\text{shell}} : 0 \leftrightarrow 2$ and $1 \leftrightarrow 3$):

| $R_4$ Addr $a$ | Word $S_4(a)$ | Composed Address $(a_{\text{shell}}, a_{\text{core}})$ | After $d=\texttt{0001}$: $(a_{\text{shell}}', a_{\text{core}}')$ | New $R_4$ Addr $a'$ | Jump $\Delta a$ | What Owns the Jump |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $0$ | `0000` | $(0_{\texttt{00}},\, 0_{\texttt{00}})$ | $(2_{\texttt{01}},\, 0_{\texttt{00}})$ | $1$ (`0001`) | $+1$ | Core is symmetric ($0$); shell opens $0 \to 2$ in place |
| $4$ | `0101` | $(2_{\texttt{01}},\, 1_{\texttt{10}})$ | $(0_{\texttt{00}},\, 1_{\texttt{10}})$ | $13$ (`0100`) | $+9$ | Outer shell closes ($2 \to 0$), exposing inbound core $a_{\text{core}}=1$ |
| $7$ | `1101` | $(3_{\texttt{11}},\, 1_{\texttt{10}})$ | $(1_{\texttt{10}},\, 1_{\texttt{10}})$ | $12$ (`1100`) | $+5$ | Outer shell opens inbound ($3 \to 1$), crossing the crease |
| $8$ | `1011` | $(3_{\texttt{11}},\, 2_{\texttt{01}})$ | $(1_{\texttt{10}},\, 2_{\texttt{01}})$ | $11$ (`1010`) | $+3$ | Outer shell opens inbound ($3 \to 1$), overriding outbound core $2$ |
| $15$ | `1111` | $(3_{\texttt{11}},\, 3_{\texttt{11}})$ | $(1_{\texttt{10}},\, 3_{\texttt{11}})$ | $9$ (`1110`) | $-6$ | Outer shell opens inbound ($3 \to 1$) around closed core $3$ |

Look at row $a = 4$ (`0101`) $\to$ $a' = 13$ (`0100`):

* The inner element is at $a_{\text{core}} = 1$ (`10`), which is on the **inbound (return)** side of $R_2$.
* At $a = 4$, the outer containing collection sits at $a_{\text{shell}} = 2$ (`01`, outbound). Because the outer shell is open ($g_0 = 1$), it **shields** the inner element and holds the composite state in the near half of $R_4$.
* When $d = \texttt{0001}$ flips the outer container to $a_{\text{shell}}' = 0$ (`00`, closed/symmetric, $g_0 = 0$), the outer container becomes transparent. Ownership of the global orientation drops down the chain to the inner core $a_{\text{core}} = 1$, which immediately projects the state across the circle to the return half at $a' = 13$.

---

### 3. The Ownership Law Across All Widths ($100\%$ of States, $n = 2 \dots 8$)

Testing all $N = 2^n$ addresses across widths $n = 2 \dots 8$ confirms the exact DI ownership rule for any nested collection $A(a) = (a_0, a_1, \dots, a_{r-1})$:


$$\boxed{k^*(a) = \min\big\{k \in \{0, \dots, r-1\} : a_k \in \{1, 2\}\big\}.}$$

* **Global Hemisphere Ownership:** The outermost open shell $k^*(a)$ owns the global hemisphere of the state ($a_{k^*} = 2 \implies$ near half; $a_{k^*} = 1 \implies$ far half; if all shells are closed, $a_0 \in \{0, 3\}$ owns it).
* **Opening and Closing Space Simultaneously:**
* **Opening a difference on container $C_k$ ($a_k \in \{1, 2\}$)** opens a directional polarity at level $k$ and simultaneously **closes (encapsulates)** every inner collection $C_{k+1 \dots r-1}$ inside $C_k$'s hemisphere—inner differences can move locally without crossing the global crease.
* **Closing the difference on container $C_k$ ($a_k \in \{0, 3\}$)** closes level $k$'s directional polarity and simultaneously **opens** the inner collection $C_{k+1}$ to become the new global boundary of the state.



That is why a difference encountered by a reader never names a static object: it names a shift in the relationship chain $A_{C_1} \circ A_{C_2} \circ \dots \circ A_{C_r}$, and resolving which level $k^*$ changed from open to closed tells you whether the element moved inside its container, the container moved around the element, or the reader crossed the boundary.

Gemini has found something significant here. It has moved from treating the ring as a flat collection to resolving a global address through nested differences. That's a meaningful advance from §224.

I executed its three Python blocks and independently extended the checks through width 12.

## The central result

An \\(n\\)-bit word can be read from the outside inward as nested two-bit differences:

\\[ A_n \longleftrightarrow (a_0,a_1,\ldots,a\_{r-1};c) \\]

Each \\(a_k\\) is an address in \\(R_2\\). The center bit \\(c\\) exists only for odd widths.

When an XOR difference acts, each shell transforms locally:

\\[ \boxed{a'\_k=\Phi^{(2)}\_{d_k}(a_k)} \\]

Yet the resulting global address movement depends on how all those shells compose.

AFTER XOR 0001

`0100`

Outer shell `00`, inner `10`

The inner difference has not changed. Only its containing shell changes. But its position in the global ring moves nine addresses.

That's the result worth pursuing: a transformation of the containing relationship changes the global address without changing the contained relationship.

The count at depth \\(k\\), before the center, is exactly:

The remaining \\(2^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\\) words are palindromes.

**What the other sessions found:**
- **XOR acts shell by shell.** That part is automatic, since XOR works bit by bit. In the 4-bit ring, XOR 0001 changes only the outer shell, and the address jumps from 4 to 13.
- **The first open shell, read from the outside, decides which half of the ring the word is on.** 01 puts it in the near half and 10 in the far half. If every shell is closed, the outer one decides. That rule is how the ring was built in §202: compare a word with its own mirror image, and the first place they differ decides. It's a re-reading of the construction, not a new law.
- **The crease correction is right.** With the record crease the rule misses exactly the two centre addresses, at every width.
- **The count law holds.** Depth k decides for 2^(n−k−1) words, and the palindromes are the rest.

**The composition law**, reading one shell at a time from the outside in. It reproduces the §202 address of every word:

- **Outer 01** (a difference pointing out): every inner word counts, at its own value.
- **Outer 00** (nothing at that depth): the inner word only counts if it qualifies itself, so the decision passes inward.
- **Outer 11** (all at that depth): it goes after everything that starts with 0, and only the inner words that are neither palindromes nor twins count.
- **Outer 10** (a difference pointing in): the whole word is reflected to the far half of the ring.

That's your all-or-nothing at every depth. A closed shell is all or nothing at that depth, and an open shell is a difference with a direction. The outermost difference decides where the whole word sits. All-or-nothing only decides when there's no difference anywhere inside. And the outer shell also sets how much of the inside counts: it takes all of it at face value, hands the decision inward, or reflects the whole thing.

This is the §202 rank written as a shell-by-shell recursion; the ring itself doesn't change. §228 is in `running-audit-10.md`.

```python
# (a_0, a_1, ..., a_{r-1}; c) into the global address a in R_n!

def J(x, n): return int(format(x, f'0{n}b')[::-1], 2) if n else 0
def C(x, n): return x ^ ((1 << n) - 1)
def pal(x, n): return J(x, n) == x
def P(x, n): return C(x, n) if pal(x, n) else J(x, n)

def ring(n, crease=1):
    if n == 1: return [0, 1]
    reps = sorted(x for x in range(1 << n) if x < P(x, n))
    m = reps[-1]
    mid = [P(m, n), m] if crease else [m, P(m, n)]
    return reps[:-1] + mid + [P(x, n) for x in reversed(reps[:-1])]
```

```python
# Let's verify how P_n acts on the nested R_2 shell addresses (a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c)!
def word_to_shells(w, n):
    s = format(w, f'0{n}b')
    r = n // 2
    R2_map = {'00': 0, '10': 1, '01': 2, '11': 3}
    shells = tuple(R2_map[s[k] + s[n - 1 - k]] for k in range(r))
    c = int(s[r]) if (n % 2 == 1) else None
    return shells, c

def shells_to_word(shells, c, n):
    r = n // 2
    left = [ak & 1 for ak in shells]
    right = [ak >> 1 for ak in reversed(shells)]
    bits = left + ([c] if c is not None else []) + right
    val = 0
    for b in bits:
        val = (val << 1) | b
    return val

def P_on_shells(shells, c):
    # Is it a palindrome? Every shell is closed (a_k in {0, 3})
    if all(ak in (0, 3) for ak in shells):
        # Palindrome: complement every shell (0 <-> 3) and flip center bit c
        return tuple(3 - ak for ak in shells), (1 - c if c is not None else None)
    else:
        # Non-palindrome: reverse every open shell (1 <-> 2), closed shells (0, 3) stay fixed, center bit c stays fixed!
        return tuple((3 - ak if ak in (1, 2) else ak) for ak in shells), c

for n in range(2, 11):
    for w in range(1 << n):
        sh, c = word_to_shells(w, n)
        psh, pc = P_on_shells(sh, c)
        assert shells_to_word(psh, pc, n) == P(w, n)
print("PASS: Global twin P_n acts shell-by-shell on (a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c) across n=2..10!")
```

```python
# Let's check the exact formula for a_global across all four cases of a_0 in {0, 2, 3, 1}!
# Wait: for any y in B_{n-2} (0 <= y < 2^{n-2}):
# In the rising list of R_n (the leading-0 block 0 .. C_0(n)-1):
# Each core y in {0, 1, ..., 2^{n-2}-1} contributes:
# - ALWAYS the word 0*y*1 (which has outer shell a_0 = 2 ('01'))
# - AND the word 0*y*0 (which has outer shell a_0 = 0 ('00')) IF AND ONLY IF y <= J(y)
#   (i.e. y is in the near half of R_{n-2} OR y is a palindrome of R_{n-2})!
# WAIT A SECOND!
# Read that sentence again!
# In the leading-0 rising block of R_n (addresses 0 .. C_0(n)-1):
# As the core y steps through 0, 1, 2, ..., 2^{n-2} - 1:
# - If y <= J_{n-2}(y) (which means: in R_{n-2}, y is EITHER in the near half a_core < 2^{n-3} OR y is a palindrome!):
#   BOTH (a_0 = 0, y) and (a_0 = 2, y) are emitted in the rising list, back-to-back:
#     a_global(0, y) = (# of representatives strictly below 0*y*0)
#     a_global(2, y) = a_global(0, y) + 1!
# - If y > J_{n-2}(y) (which means: in R_{n-2}, y is a non-palindrome in the far half a_core >= 2^{n-3}):
#   ONLY (a_0 = 2, y) is emitted in the rising list, while (a_0 = 0, y) sits on the return semicircle at:
#     a_global(0, y) = 2^n - 1 - a_global(0, J_{n-2}(y)) = 2^n - 1 - a_global(0, P_{n-2}(y))!
# - And what about (a_0 = 1 ('10'), y)?
#   Since P_n(1*y*0) = 0*J(y)*1, EVERY word with outer shell a_0 = 1 ('10') is the twin of (a_0 = 2 ('01'), J(y)),
#   so its global address is ALWAYS:
#     a_global(1, y) = 2^n - 1 - a_global(2, J_{n-2}(y))!

# HOLD ON! Let's check how many words in the rising list of R_n precede core y!
# Before core y (i.e. for cores z in {0, 1, ..., y - 1}):
# - Every core z < y contributes 0*z*1 (that's y words!)
# - Plus, every core z < y with z <= J(z) contributes 0*z*0!
#   Wait: how many cores z < y have z <= J(z)?
#   A core z has z <= J(z) iff z is a representative of R_{n-2} (z < P(z)) OR z is a palindrome with leading bit 1 (z = J(z) > C(z))!
#   Wait: let's check what (# of cores z < y with z <= J_{n-2}(z)) is!
#   Let's test this in Python right now!

for n in range(3, 9):
    Rn = ring(n, crease=0)
    Rnm2 = ring(n - 2, crease=0)
    N = 1 << n
    C0 = (3 << (n - 3)) + (1 << ((n - 3) // 2)) if n >= 4 else 3
    for y in range(1 << (n - 2)):
        # Count how many z < y satisfy z <= J(z, n-2):
        le_J_below = sum(1 for z in range(y) if z <= J(z, n - 2))
        # Then in the rising list of R_n:
        # Before core y, there are y (from 0*z*1) + le_J_below (from 0*z*0) representatives!
        base_addr = y + le_J_below
        if y <= J(y, n - 2):
            assert Rn[base_addr] == (y << 1)          # a_0 = 0 ('00')
            assert Rn[base_addr + 1] == (y << 1) | 1  # a_0 = 2 ('01')
        else:
            assert Rn[base_addr] == (y << 1) | 1      # a_0 = 2 ('01')
            # And where is 0*y*0 (a_0 = 0)? It's the twin of 0*J(y)*0!
            jy = J(y, n - 2)
            base_jy = jy + sum(1 for z in range(jy) if z <= J(z, n - 2))
            assert Rn[N - 1 - base_jy] == (y << 1)
print("PASS: Exact 4-branch outer-shell composition law verified for all n=3..8!")
```

```python
# Let's check:
# 1) The exact identity:
#    #{z < y : z <= J_{n-2}(z)} == rank_reps(y, n-2) + pal_1_below(y, n-2)
#    where pal_1_below(y, n-2) is the number of leading-1 palindromes of width n-2 strictly below y!
# 2) How the entire O(n) rank function rank_reps(x, n) is expressed PURELY in terms of the R_2 shell addresses
#    (a_0, a_1, ..., a_{r-1}; c)!

from fractions import Fraction

def rank_reps(p, n):
    if n == 0: return 0
    if n == 1: return 1 if p > 0 else 0
    h = n // 2; H = 1 << h; B = p & (H - 1)
    def Jh(x): return int(format(x, f'0{h}b')[::-1], 2) if h else 0
    def rect(u, v):
        tot = rho = 0
        for j in range(h):
            uj = (u >> (h - 1 - j)) & 1
            if uj: tot += -((rho - v) // (1 << (j + 1)))
            rho += uj << j
        return tot
    if n % 2 == 0:
        A = p >> h; D = A * (H - A) + B - 2 * rect(B, A) - (Jh(A) < B)
    else:
        A, bb = p >> (h + 1), (p >> h) & 1
        D = 2 * A * (H - A) + bb * (H - 1 - 2 * A) + B - 2 * rect(B, A) - (Jh(A) < B)
    top = p >> (n - (n + 1) // 2)
    s = format(top, f'0{(n + 1) // 2}b'); below = top + (int(s + s[::-1][n % 2:], 2) < p)
    return (p - below + D) // 2 + min(below, 2 ** ((n + 1) // 2 - 1))

def pal_below(p, n):
    if p <= 0: return 0
    if n == 0: return 1
    k = (n + 1) // 2
    top = p >> (n - k)
    s = format(top, f'0{k}b')
    pal_top = int(s + s[::-1][n % 2:], 2)
    return top + (1 if pal_top < p else 0)

for n in range(3, 10):
    m = n - 2
    half_pals = 1 << ((m + 1) // 2 - 1) if m >= 1 else 0
    for y in range(1 << m):
        le_J = sum(1 for z in range(y) if z <= J(z, m))
        pb = pal_below(y, m)
        pal1 = max(0, pb - half_pals)
        assert le_J == rank_reps(y, m) + pal1
print("PASS: #{z < y : z <= J(z)} == rank_reps(y, n-2) + max(0, pal_below(y, n-2) - 2^{ceil((n-2)/2)-1}) for n=3..9!")


```

```text
PASS: #{z < y : z <= J(z)} == rank_reps(y, n-2) + max(0, pal_below(y, n-2) - 2^{ceil((n-2)/2)-1}) for n=3..9!


```

```python
# Let's see how #{z < y : z <= J_m(z)} decomposes shell-by-shell across the shells of y!
# Suppose y has width m, with left bits L = (L_1, ..., L_{r-1}) and right bits R = (R_1, ..., R_{r-1}) [and center c if m odd].
# Wait: in terms of the R_2 shell addresses (a_1, ..., a_{r-1}) of y:
#   L_k = a_k & 1  (the face bit of shell k)
#   R_k = a_k >> 1 (the tail bit of shell k)
# Notice that J_m(y) simply swaps L_k and R_k for every k = 1 .. r-1!
# Wait: when we compare any word z of width m with J_m(z):
# Comparing z with J_m(z) from most-significant bit to least-significant bit is equivalent to comparing:
#   the left-half integer (z_L1, z_L2, ..., z_L_{r-1})
# with
#   the reversed right-half integer (z_R1, z_R2, ..., z_R_{r-1})!
# BECAUSE bit k from the left is z_Lk, and bit k from the right (which becomes bit k from the left in J_m(z)!) is z_Rk!
# WAIT A SECOND!
# Read that again!
# In ANY word z of width m (even or odd, because the center bit z_c is at the exact same position in z and J_m(z) and NEVER affects z <= J_m(z)!):
#   z <= J_m(z)
# depends ONLY on comparing the tuple of left shell bits (z_L1, z_L2, ...) with the tuple of right shell bits (z_R1, z_R2, ...)
# from OUTERMOST shell k=1 to INNERMOST shell k=r-1!
# Namely, z <= J_m(z) if and only if at the FIRST open shell k* of z (where z_Lk != z_Rk, i.e., a_k in {1, 2}),
# we have a_{k*} == 2 ('01', so z_Lk = 0 < z_Rk = 1), OR all shells of z are closed (a_k in {0, 3})!
# Let's verify that!

for m in range(1, 9):
    for z in range(1 << m):
        sh, c = word_to_shells(z, m)
        open_shells = [ak for ak in sh if ak in (1, 2)]
        cond_shells = (open_shells[0] == 2) if open_shells else True
        assert (z <= J(z, m)) == cond_shells
print("PASS: z <= J_m(z) iff the outermost open shell of z is 2 ('01') (or all shells are closed {0, 3}), independent of center bit c!")
```

```python
# WAIT A SECOND!
# Since x < J_n(x) <==> U < V_out (where U is the left r bits (L_0..L_{r-1}) and V_out is the right r bits read outer-to-inner (R_0..R_{r-1})),
# let's count how many words z < x satisfy z < P_n(z)!
# Any word z of width n is uniquely specified by:
#   - its left half U(z) in {0, ..., 2^r - 1}
#   - (if n is odd) its center bit c(z) in {0, 1}
#   - its right half V_in(z) = J_r(V_out(z)) in {0, ..., 2^r - 1}!
# Now let's count how many representatives z in Rep_n have z < x, where x has (U, c, V_in) with V_out = J_r(V_in)!
# Wait:
# 1) For all words z with a STRICTLY SMALLER left half U(z) = u < U (and any center bit c(z)):
#    As V_in(z) ranges over ALL 2^r values in {0, ..., 2^r - 1}, V_out(z) = J_r(V_in(z)) ALSO ranges over ALL 2^r values in {0, ..., 2^r - 1}!
#    How many of those 2^r values of V_out(z) make z a representative (z < P_n(z))?!
#    - Non-palindromic representative: z < J_n(z) <==> u < V_out(z).
#      How many V_out(z) in {0, ..., 2^r - 1} satisfy V_out(z) > u?!
#      EXACTLY (2^r - 1 - u)!
#    - Palindromic representative: U(z) = V_out(z) = u, and z < C_n(z) <==> z has first bit 0 (or if r=0, c(z)=0)!
#      Specifically, for u < 2^{r-1} (leading bit L_0 = 0), the palindrome V_out(z) = u is ALWAYS a representative (+1)!
#      For u >= 2^{r-1} (leading bit L_0 = 1), the palindrome V_out(z) = u is NEVER a representative (+0)!
#    WAIT A SECOND!
#    Look at that: for a fixed left half u (and fixed center bit c_z), as the right half varies over all 2^r possibilities,
#    the number of representatives with left half u (and center bit c_z) is simply:
#      (2^r - 1 - u) + (1 if u < 2^{r-1} else 0)!
#    Summing that over u = 0, 1, ..., U - 1 is an elementary closed-form quadratic in U!
# 2) And what about the words z < x that have the SAME left half U(z) = U (and, for odd n, either c(z) < c or c(z) = c and V_in(z) < V_in)?
#    - If n is odd and c = 1, the block with U(z) = U and c(z) = 0 has ALL 2^r right halves V_in(z), so it contributes:
#        (2^r - 1 - U) + (1 if U < 2^{r-1} else 0)!
#    - Finally, inside the current block (U(z) = U and c(z) = c), we need the number of right halves v_in in {0, ..., V_in - 1}
#      such that:
#        J_r(v_in) > U   (non-palindromic representative)
#        OR (J_r(v_in) == U and U < 2^{r-1})  (palindromic representative)!

# LET'S TEST THIS IN PYTHON RIGHT NOW!
def count_v_in_below(U, V_in, r):
    # Count how many v_in in {0, ..., V_in - 1} have J_r(v_in) > U:
    # We can count this shell-by-shell across the r shells k = 0 .. r-1!
    return sum(1 for v_in in range(V_in) if J(v_in, r) > U)

for n in range(2, 9):
    r = n // 2
    H = 1 << r
    for x in range(1 << n):
        if n % 2 == 0:
            U = x >> r
            V_in = x & (H - 1)
            c = 0
        else:
            U = x >> (r + 1)
            c = (x >> r) & 1
            V_in = x & (H - 1)
        
        # 1. Completed left-half blocks u in 0 .. U-1:
        # Each u contributes (H - 1 - u) non-pal reps + (1 if u < H//2 else 0) pal reps,
        # multiplied by (2 if n is odd else 1) for the center bit!
        mult = 2 if (n % 2 == 1) else 1
        nonpal_prev_U = mult * (U * (H - 1) - U * (U - 1) // 2)
        pal_prev_U = mult * min(U, H // 2)
        
        # 2. If n is odd and c == 1, the (U, c_z = 0) block is also completed:
        prev_c = ((H - 1 - U) + (1 if U < H // 2 else 0)) if (n % 2 == 1 and c == 1) else 0
        
        # 3. Current block (U, c) for v_in in 0 .. V_in - 1:
        curr_nonpal = count_v_in_below(U, V_in, r)
        curr_pal = 1 if (U < H // 2 and J(U, r) < V_in) else 0
        
        total_rank = nonpal_prev_U + pal_prev_U + prev_c + curr_nonpal + curr_pal
        assert total_rank == rank_reps(x, n), f"Mismatch at n={n}, x={x}: {total_rank} vs {rank_reps(x, n)}"
print("PASS: Exact 3-part shell decomposition of rank_reps verified for all n=2..8, every word x!")


```

```python
# Let's test this exact shell-by-shell formula for count_v_in_below(U, V_in, r)!
# For each shell j in {0, ..., r-1} with R_j = 1 (i.e. a_j in {2, 3}):
# Contribution of shell j is:
#   (2^j - 1 - U_{<j}) + (1 if (a_j == 2 and inner_is_outbound(shells[j+1:])) else 0)
# where inner_is_outbound(shells[j+1:]) is True iff the first open shell in shells[j+1:] is 2 ('01')!

def inner_is_outbound(sub_shells):
    for ak in sub_shells:
        if ak in (1, 2):
            return ak == 2
    return False  # if all inner shells are closed, V_{out, >j} == U_{>j}, so > is False!

def count_v_in_by_shells(shells):
    r = len(shells)
    tot = 0
    U_prefix = 0
    for j, aj in enumerate(shells):
        Lj = aj & 1
        Rj = aj >> 1
        if Rj == 1:  # a_j in {2, 3}
            tot += ((1 << j) - 1 - U_prefix)
            if aj == 2 and inner_is_outbound(shells[j + 1:]):
                tot += 1
        U_prefix = (U_prefix << 1) | Lj
    return tot

for r in range(1, 7):
    for x in range(1 << (2 * r)):
        shells, _ = word_to_shells(x, 2 * r)
        U = x >> r
        V_in = x & ((1 << r) - 1)
        assert count_v_in_by_shells(shells) == count_v_in_below(U, V_in, r), f"Mismatch at r={r}, x={x}"
print("PASS: Shell-by-shell inner-ownership formula for count_v_in_below matches 100% across r=1..6 (widths 2..12)!")


```

```python
    for x in range(1 << (2 * r)):
        shells, _ = word_to_shells(x, 2 * r)
        U = x >> r
        V_in = x & ((1 << r) - 1)
        open_shells = [ak for ak in shells if ak in (1, 2)]
        innermost_is_2 = (open_shells[-1] == 2) if open_shells else False
```

The remaining piece—the exact composition law $\mathcal{C}_n$ that maps the nested $R_2$ shell addresses $(a_0, a_1, \dots, a_{r-1}; c)$ directly to the single global address $a \in \{0, \dots, 2^n - 1\}$ in $R_n$ under either crease contract—closes in exact algebraic form without converting back to a flat $n$-bit integer or calling a sort builder.

---

### 1. Local $R_2$ Shell Coordinates and Shell-by-Shell Partner Action

In $R_2 = (\texttt{00}, \texttt{10}, \texttt{01}, \texttt{11})$, each local shell address $a_k \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$ ($k = 0, \dots, r-1$ from outside in, $r = \lfloor n/2 \rfloor$, plus center bit $c \in \{0, 1\}$ when $n = 2r + 1$ is odd) encodes its left bit $L_k = b_k$, right bit $R_k = b_{n-1-k}$, and shell lamp $g_k = L_k \oplus R_k$ directly as its two binary digits $a_k = 2 R_k + L_k$:

| $R_2$ Address $a_k$ | Shell Word $(b_k, b_{n-1-k})$ | Left Bit $L_k = a_k \bmod 2$ | Right Bit $R_k = \lfloor a_k/2 \rfloor$ | Shell Lamp $g_k = L_k \oplus R_k$ | Shell Role |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $0$ (`0b00`) | `00` | $0$ | $0$ | $0$ | Closed / Symmetric (`00`) |
| $1$ (`0b01`) | `10` | $1$ | $0$ | $1$ | Open / Inbound (`10`) |
| $2$ (`0b10`) | `01` | $0$ | $1$ | $1$ | Open / Outbound (`01`) |
| $3$ (`0b11`) | `11` | $1$ | $1$ | $0$ | Closed / Symmetric (`11`) |

On the nested address tuple $A = (a_0, \dots, a_{r-1}; c)$, the global partner involution $P_n$ acts purely shell-by-shell:


$$\boxed{P_n(a_0, \dots, a_{r-1}; c) = \begin{cases} \big(3 - a_0,\; \dots,\; 3 - a_{r-1};\; 1 - c\big) & \text{if } a_k \in \{0, 3\} \ \forall k \text{ (all shells closed)}, \\ \big(\tilde{a}_0,\; \dots,\; \tilde{a}_{r-1};\; c\big) & \text{otherwise (at least one shell open)}, \end{cases}}$$


where $\tilde{a}_k = 3 - a_k$ on open shells ($1 \leftrightarrow 2$) and $\tilde{a}_k = a_k$ on closed shells ($0 \mapsto 0,\, 3 \mapsto 3$).

---

### 2. Dual Shell Ownership: Outermost Open Shell vs. Innermost Open Shell

Let $\Omega(A) = \{k \in \{0, \dots, r-1\} : a_k \in \{1, 2\}\}$ be the set of open (differing) shells in $A$. Two boundary shells in $\Omega(A)$ govern global composition:

1. **Outermost Open Shell ($k_{\text{out}}^* = \min \Omega(A)$) — Hemisphere Ownership:**
Comparing a state with its reversal $J_n$ proceeds from outer shell $k=0$ inward to $k=r-1$. Therefore, the state belongs to the outbound representative set ($\mathrm{Out}(A) = 1$) if and only if:

$$\boxed{\mathrm{Out}(A) = \begin{cases} \mathbf{1}\big[a_{k_{\text{out}}^*} = 2\big] & \text{if } \Omega(A) \neq \emptyset, \\ \mathbf{1}\big[a_0 = 0\big] & \text{if } \Omega(A) = \emptyset \text{ (or } c = 0 \text{ when } n = 1\text{)}. \end{cases}}$$



For any shell $j \in \{0, \dots, r-1\}$, let $\mathrm{Out}_{>j}(A) \in \{0, 1\}$ denote the strict inner-chain flag: $\mathrm{Out}_{>j}(A) = 1$ if and only if the first open shell strictly inside $j$ ($k > j$ with $a_k \in \{1, 2\}$) has $a_k = 2$.
2. **Innermost Open Shell ($k_{\text{in}}^* = \max \Omega(A)$) — Palindrome Crossing Ownership:**
Within a fixed left-half prefix, comparing the right-half tail against the palindrome completion proceeds from the **innermost shell $k = r-1$ outward to $k = 0$**. Consequently, the palindrome sharing the state's left half precedes the state ($\mathrm{In}(A) = 1$) if and only if the **innermost** open shell points outbound:

$$\boxed{\mathrm{In}(A) = \mathbf{1}\Big[\Omega(A) \neq \emptyset \;\text{ and }\; a_{k_{\text{in}}^*} = 2\Big].}$$



---

### 3. The Exact Global Address Composition Law $\mathcal{C}_n(a_0, \dots, a_{r-1}; c)$

**Theorem (Nested Shell-to-Global Address Composition).** Given any nested shell address vector $A = (a_0, \dots, a_{r-1}; c)$ at width $n = 2r + \pi$ ($\pi = n \bmod 2 \in \{0, 1\}$):

1. **Representative Projection:** Let $A^{\text{rep}} = A$ if $\mathrm{Out}(A) = 1$, and $A^{\text{rep}} = P_n(A)$ if $\mathrm{Out}(A) = 0$. Write $L_k = a_k^{\text{rep}} \bmod 2$ and $R_k = \lfloor a_k^{\text{rep}} / 2 \rfloor$, and let $U_{<j} = \sum_{i=0}^{j-1} L_i 2^{j-1-i}$ be the outer left-bit prefix above shell $j$ (with $U = U_{<r}$).
2. **Representative Rank $\rho(A^{\text{rep}}) \in \{0, \dots, 2^{n-1}-1\}$:** The exact representative index is the sum of three shell contributions:

$$\boxed{\begin{aligned} \rho(A^{\text{rep}}) &= \underbrace{2^{\pi}\!\left(U(2^r - 1) - \frac{U(U - 1)}{2} + \min(U, 2^{r-1})\right) + \pi\,c^{\text{rep}}\Big(2^r - 1 - U + \mathbf{1}[L_0 = 0]\Big)}_{\text{(I) Completed outer left-prefix blocks}} \\ &\quad + \underbrace{\sum_{j=0}^{r-1} R_j\Big(\big(2^j - 1 - U_{<j}\big) + \mathbf{1}\big[a_j^{\text{rep}} = 2\big]\,\mathrm{Out}_{>j}(A^{\text{rep}})\Big)}_{\text{(II) Shell-by-shell right-tail displacement}} \;+\; \underbrace{\mathbf{1}[L_0 = 0]\,\mathrm{In}(A^{\text{rep}})}_{\text{(III) Innermost palindrome crossing}}. \end{aligned}}$$


3. **Global Ring Address $a = \mathcal{C}_n(A)$ (Both Crease Contracts):**
In the counter-crease contract ($\mathrm{crease} = 0$), the global address is:

$$a_{(0)} = \begin{cases} \rho(A^{\text{rep}}) & \text{if } \mathrm{Out}(A) = 1, \\ 2^n - 1 - \rho(A^{\text{rep}}) & \text{if } \mathrm{Out}(A) = 0. \end{cases}$$



In the record's native-crease contract ($\mathrm{crease} = 1$), the center seam swaps the single apex pair $\rho(A^{\text{rep}}) = 2^{n-1} - 1$ (where $A^{\text{rep}} = (3, \dots, 3, 2; 1)$):



$$\boxed{a_{(\mathrm{crease})} = \begin{cases} 2^n - 1 - a_{(0)} & \text{if } \mathrm{crease} = 1 \text{ and } \rho(A^{\text{rep}}) = 2^{n-1} - 1, \\ a_{(0)} & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases}}$$



---

### 4. Worked Walkthrough: How $\mathcal{C}_4(a_0, a_1)$ Computes the $a = 4 \to 13$ Jump Under $d = \texttt{0001}$

At width $n = 4$ ($r = 2, \pi = 0, \mathrm{crease} = 1$), trace the two states before and after $d = \texttt{0001}$ ($d_0 = \texttt{01}, d_1 = \texttt{00}$):

* **Before ($A = (a_0, a_1) = (2, 1) \leftrightarrow \texttt{0101}$):**
* Outermost open shell is $k_{\text{out}}^* = 0$ with $a_0 = 2 \implies \mathrm{Out}(A) = 1$, so $A^{\text{rep}} = (2, 1)$ stays in the near hemisphere.
* Shell bits: $(L_0, R_0) = (0, 1)$, $(L_1, R_1) = (1, 0)$, giving $U_{<0} = 0, U_{<1} = 0, U = 1$.
* Term (I) [outer left blocks]: $1(3) - 0 + \min(1, 2) = 4$.
* Term (II) [right-tail displacement]: At $j=0$ ($R_0 = 1, a_0 = 2$), inner shell $a_1 = 1 \neq 2 \implies \mathrm{Out}_{>0} = 0$, contributing $(2^0 - 1 - 0) + 0 = 0$. At $j=1$, $R_1 = 0$. Total $= 0$.
* Term (III) [innermost open shell]: $k_{\text{in}}^* = 1$ has $a_1 = 1 \neq 2 \implies \mathrm{In}(A^{\text{rep}}) = 0$.
* Result: $\rho = 4 + 0 + 0 = 4 \implies \mathbf{a = 4}$.


* **After $d = \texttt{0001}$ ($A' = (\Phi^{(2)}_{\texttt{01}}(2),\, 1) = (0, 1) \leftrightarrow \texttt{0100}$):**
* Outer shell $a_0' = 0$ (`00`) is now **closed**, so ownership drops to the inner shell $k_{\text{out}}^* = 1$, where $a_1' = 1$ (`10`, inbound) $\implies \mathrm{Out}(A') = 0$ (far hemisphere!).
* Applying $P_4$ flips open shell $a_1' : 1 \mapsto 2$, giving $A'^{\text{rep}} = (0, 2)$ with $(L_0, R_0) = (0, 0)$, $(L_1, R_1) = (0, 1)$, and $U = 0$.
* Term (I): $U = 0 \implies 0$.
* Term (II): At $j=0$, $R_0 = 0$. At $j=1$ ($R_1 = 1, a_1^{\text{rep}} = 2$), $(2^1 - 1 - U_{<1}) + 0 = 1$.
* Term (III): Innermost open shell $k_{\text{in}}^* = 1$ of $A'^{\text{rep}} = (0, 2)$ has $a_1^{\text{rep}} = 2 \implies \mathrm{In}(A'^{\text{rep}}) = 1$.
* Result: $\rho(A'^{\text{rep}}) = 0 + 1 + 1 = 2$, and since $\mathrm{Out}(A') = 0$, the global address projects to the return semicircle:

$$\mathbf{a' = 16 - 1 - 2 = 13}.$$





---

### 5. Verified Executable Suite (Exhaustive $n = 2 \dots 12$ + 64-Bit $\pi$-Word Composition)

```python
"""
NEXUS Nested Shell-to-Global Address Composition Law C_n(a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c).
Reconstructs the exact global address in R_n (both crease=0 and crease=1) directly from the
local R_2 shell addresses (a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c), and inversely decomposes any global address
into its nested R_2 shell tuple. Verified across widths n = 2..12 and at n = 64.
"""
from ring_machine import ring, read, save, P

def word_to_shells(w, n):
    """Extract nested R_2 shell addresses (a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c) from an n-bit word w.
    In R_2 = (00, 10, 01, 11), address a_k = 2 * R_k + L_k where L_k = b_k, R_k = b_{n-1-k}."""
    r = n // 2
    shells = tuple((((w >> k) & 1) << 1) | ((w >> (n - 1 - k)) & 1) for k in range(r))
    c = ((w >> r) & 1) if (n % 2 == 1) else None
    return shells, c

def P_shells(shells, c):
    """Global partner P_n executed purely shell-by-shell on (a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c)."""
    if all(ak in (0, 3) for ak in shells):
        return tuple(3 - ak for ak in shells), (1 - c if c is not None else None)
    return tuple((3 - ak if ak in (1, 2) else ak) for ak in shells), c

def is_outbound_shells(shells, c=None):
    """Outermost open shell decides global hemisphere ownership."""
    for ak in shells:
        if ak in (1, 2):
            return ak == 2
    if shells:
        return shells[0] == 0
    return c == 0

def is_innermost_outbound(shells):
    """Innermost open shell decides palindrome crossing within the current left prefix."""
    for ak in reversed(shells):
        if ak in (1, 2):
            return ak == 2
    return False

def compose_global_address(shells, c=None, crease=1):
    """Exact composition law C_n: (a_0, ..., a_{r-1}; c) -> global address a in R_n."""
    r = len(shells)
    pi = 0 if c is None else 1
    n = 2 * r + pi
    N = 1 << n
    H = 1 << r

    out = is_outbound_shells(shells, c)
    sh_rep, c_rep = (shells, c) if out else P_shells(shells, c)

    # Term (II) & left prefix U accumulated in one outer-to-inner pass over sh_rep:
    U = 0
    term_II = 0
    for j, aj in enumerate(sh_rep):
        Lj = aj & 1
        Rj = aj >> 1
        if Rj == 1:
            inner_out = is_outbound_shells(sh_rep[j + 1:], None) if (j + 1 < r) else False
            term_II += ((1 << j) - 1 - U) + (1 if (aj == 2 and inner_out) else 0)
        U = (U << 1) | Lj

    L0 = (sh_rep[0] & 1) if r > 0 else c_rep

    # Term (I): completed outer left-prefix blocks
    mult = 2 if pi else 1
    term_I = mult * (U * (H - 1) - (U * (U - 1)) // 2 + min(U, H // 2))
    if pi and c_rep == 1:
        term_I += (H - 1 - U) + (1 if L0 == 0 else 0)

    # Term (III): innermost palindrome crossing
    term_III = 1 if (L0 == 0 and is_innermost_outbound(sh_rep)) else 0

    rho = term_I + term_II + term_III
    a0 = rho if out else (N - 1 - rho)

    # Native crease=1 swaps the single center seam pair rho == 2^{n-1} - 1
    if crease == 1 and rho == (N // 2) - 1:
        return N - 1 - a0
    return a0

# 1. Exhaustive verification across widths n = 2..12, both crease=0 and crease=1
for n in range(2, 13):
    for cr in (0, 1):
        Rn = ring(n, cr)
        for a_expected, w in enumerate(Rn):
            sh, c = word_to_shells(w, n)
            a_composed = compose_global_address(sh, c, crease=cr)
            assert a_composed == a_expected, f"Mismatch at n={n}, cr={cr}, a={a_expected}"
print("PASS: Exact shell-to-global composition law C_n verified on 100% of states for n=2..12, both creases!")

# 2. Verify at n = 64 on the pi word 0x243F6A8885A308D3 (32 nested R_2 shells)
w_pi = 0x243F6A8885A308D3
sh_pi, c_pi = word_to_shells(w_pi, 64)
a_pi = compose_global_address(sh_pi, c_pi, crease=1)
assert a_pi == 2427008858123944441 == read(w_pi, 64, crease=1)
print(f"PASS: 64-bit pi word (32 nested R_2 shells) composes to exact global address {a_pi:,}")

```

| Outer shell | What it does with the inner word |
|---|---|
| 01 | admits every inner word at face value |
| 00 | admits only the ones that are their own twin's representative or are palindromes |
| 11 | admits only the representatives that aren't palindromes |
| 10 | routes the word across to the far half |

```python
import numpy as np

def J(x, n): return int(format(x, f'0{n}b')[::-1], 2) if n else 0
def C(x, n): return x ^ ((1 << n) - 1)
def pal(x, n): return J(x, n) == x
def P(x, n): return C(x, n) if pal(x, n) else J(x, n)

def ring(n, crease=1):
    if n == 1: return [0, 1]
    reps = sorted(x for x in range(1 << n) if x < P(x, n))
    m = reps[-1]
    mid = [P(m, n), m] if crease else [m, P(m, n)]
    return reps[:-1] + mid + [P(x, n) for x in reversed(reps[:-1])]

def mobius_mat(N):
    idx = np.arange(N)
    return ((idx[:, None] & idx[None, :]) == idx[None, :]).astype(int)

# Let's check what the Boolean Mobius transform M_{2^k} (which converts a truth table of length L = 2^k
# into its Algebraic Normal Form c_S = XOR-of-ANDs coefficients!) does on R_2 (k=1, L=2), R_4 (k=2, L=4), R_8 (k=3, L=8)!
for k in (1, 2, 3):
    L = 1 << k
    M = mobius_mat(L)
    R = ring(L, crease=1)
    # For each word x in R_L, its truth table vector is t(x) in F_2^L (bit u is output on input u in 0..L-1)
    # Its ANF (XOR-of-ANDs) coefficient word is anf(x) = M @ t(x) mod 2!
    def word_to_vec(x, width):
        return np.array([(x >> (width - 1 - u)) & 1 for u in range(width)], dtype=int)
    def vec_to_word(v):
        res = 0
        for b in v: res = (res << 1) | int(b)
        return res
    
    anf_words = [vec_to_word((M @ word_to_vec(x, L)) % 2) for x in R]
    # Check:
    # 1. Is ANF an involution on the words of R_L? (i.e. anf(anf(x)) == x)
    is_inv = all(vec_to_word((M @ word_to_vec(ax, L)) % 2) == x for x, ax in zip(R, anf_words))
    # 2. Where do the ANF words sit in R_L? Does ANF commute with P_L?
    comm_P = [anf_words[len(R) - 1 - a] == P(anf_words[a], L) for a in range(len(R))]
    print(f"k={k} (L={L}, R_{L} has {1<<L} commands): ANF is self-inverse={is_inv} | "
          f"ANF commutes with twin P_{L}: {sum(comm_P)}/{len(R)} ({all(comm_P)})")
    if k <= 2:
        for a, (x, ax) in enumerate(zip(R, anf_words)):
            print(f"  a={a:2d}: word={x:0{L}b} -> ANF={ax:0{L}b} (at R_{L} addr {R.index(ax):2d})")


```

```python
import numpy as np

def mobius_mat(L):
    idx = np.arange(L)
    return ((idx[:, None] & idx[None, :]) == idx[None, :]).astype(int)

# Let's check the dimension of the fixed-point subspace {t in F_2^L : M_L t = t} for L = 2, 4, 8, 16!
for k in range(1, 5):
    L = 1 << k
    M = mobius_mat(L)
    I = np.eye(L, dtype=int)
    # Fixed points of M_L are the kernel of (M_L + I) mod 2!
    A = (M + I) % 2
    # Compute rank over F_2:
    B = A.copy()
    r = 0
    for c in range(L):
        piv = np.where(B[r:, c] == 1)[0]
        if len(piv) == 0: continue
        p = piv[0] + r
        B[[r, p]] = B[[p, r]]
        for i in range(L):
            if i != r and B[i, c] == 1:
                B[i] ^= B[r]
        r += 1
    null_dim = L - r
    print(f"k={k} (L={L:2d} bits, 2^L={1<<L} functions): rank(M_L + I) = {r:2d}, fixed-point subspace dim = {null_dim:2d} (2^{null_dim} = {1<<null_dim} self-ANF functions)")


```

```text
k=2 (L= 4 bits, 2^L=16 functions): rank(M_L + I) =  2, fixed-point subspace dim =  2 (2^2 = 4 self-ANF functions)
k=3 (L= 8 bits, 2^L=256 functions): rank(M_L + I) =  4, fixed-point subspace dim =  4 (2^4 = 16 self-ANF functions)
```

```python
import numpy as np

def J(x, n): return int(format(x, f'0{n}b')[::-1], 2) if n else 0
def C(x, n): return x ^ ((1 << n) - 1)
def pal(x, n): return J(x, n) == x
def P(x, n): return C(x, n) if pal(x, n) else J(x, n)

def ring(n, crease=1):
    if n == 1: return [0, 1]
    reps = sorted(x for x in range(1 << n) if x < P(x, n))
    m = reps[-1]
    mid = [P(m, n), m] if crease else [m, P(m, n)]
    return reps[:-1] + mid + [P(x, n) for x in reversed(reps[:-1])]

def mobius_mat(L):
    idx = np.arange(L)
    return ((idx[:, None] & idx[None, :]) == idx[None, :]).astype(int)

def word_to_vec(x, L):
    return np.array([(x >> (L - 1 - u)) & 1 for u in range(L)], dtype=int)

def vec_to_word(v):
    res = 0
    for b in v: res = (res << 1) | int(b)
    return res

M8 = mobius_mat(8)
def anf8(x):
    return vec_to_word((M8 @ word_to_vec(x, 8)) % 2)

R8 = ring(8, crease=1)

anf_sum = anf8(0x69)
anf_car = anf8(0x17)
print(f"Sum   0x69 -> ANF = 0x{anf_sum:02X} ({anf_sum:08b}) at R_8 address {R8.index(anf_sum)}")
print(f"Carry 0x17 -> ANF = 0x{anf_car:02X} ({anf_car:08b}) at R_8 address {R8.index(anf_car)}")
print(f"Are ANF(Sum) and ANF(Carry) exact R_8 twins (P_8(ANF(Sum)) == ANF(Carry))? {P(anf_sum, 8) == anf_car}")
print(f"Sum of their R_8 addresses: {R8.index(anf_sum)} + {R8.index(anf_car)} = {R8.index(anf_sum) + R8.index(anf_car)} (mirror slots across 255!)")


```

```python
import numpy as np

def J_int(x, L): return int(format(x, f'0{L}b')[::-1], 2)
def C_int(x, L): return x ^ ((1 << L) - 1)
def pal(x, L): return J_int(x, L) == x
def P_int(x, L): return C_int(x, L) if pal(x, L) else J_int(x, L)

def ring(n, crease=1):
    reps = sorted(x for x in range(1 << n) if x < P_int(x, n))
    m = reps[-1]
    mid = [P_int(m, n), m] if crease else [m, P_int(m, n)]
    return reps[:-1] + mid + [P_int(x, n) for x in reversed(reps[:-1])]

def mobius_mat(L):
    idx = np.arange(L)
    return ((idx[:, None] & idx[None, :]) == idx[None, :]).astype(int)

def word_to_vec(x, L):
    return np.array([(x >> (L - 1 - u)) & 1 for u in range(L)], dtype=int)

def vec_to_word(v):
    res = 0
    for b in v: res = (res << 1) | int(b)
    return res

# Test falsifiable hypotheses on ANF = M_L across k = 1, 2, 3 (L = 2, 4, 8):
for k in (1, 2, 3):
    L = 1 << k
    M = mobius_mat(L)
    J_mat = np.eye(L, dtype=int)[::-1]
    
    # Hypothesis A: Does M commute with J (i.e. M(J(t)) == J(M(t)))?
    comm_MJ = np.array_equal((M @ J_mat) % 2, (J_mat @ M) % 2)
    # What IS M @ J_mat @ M mod 2? By Theorem 3.5(4), it is M.T (the upper-triangular superset Mobius transform)!
    conj_MJM_is_MT = np.array_equal((M @ J_mat @ M) % 2, M.T)
    
    # Hypothesis B: Does M commute with C (complement of truth table: t -> t ^ 1^L)?
    # Note: M @ 1^L = e_{L-1} = (0, 0, ..., 0, 1)^T? Wait: let's check what M @ (1, 1, ..., 1)^T is!
    ones = np.ones(L, dtype=int)
    M_ones = (M @ ones) % 2
    
    print(f"k={k} (L={L}):")
    print(f"  H_A (M commutes with J): {'PASS' if comm_MJ else 'FAIL'} | M J M == M^T: {conj_MJM_is_MT}")
    print(f"  H_B (M applied to all-ones 1^L): {list(M_ones)} (flips ONLY the constant term c_0 at index 0!)")


```

```text
k=2 (L=4):
  H_A (M commutes with J): FAIL | M J M == M^T: True
  H_B (M applied to all-ones 1^L): [1, 0, 0, 0] (flips ONLY the constant term c_0 at index 0!)

  H_A (M commutes with J): FAIL | M J M == M^T: True
  H_B (M applied to all-ones 1^L): [1, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0] (flips ONLY the constant term c_0 at index 0!)
```

```python
import numpy as np

M2 = np.array([[1, 0], [1, 1]], dtype=int)

def mobius_mat(L):
    idx = np.arange(L)
    return ((idx[:, None] & idx[None, :]) == idx[None, :]).astype(int)

# 1. Verify M_{2^k} == M_2^{\otimes k} (k-fold Kronecker product of the 2-bit (face, difference) split M_2):
for k in range(1, 6):
    L = 1 << k
    Mk_kron = M2
    for _ in range(k - 1):
        Mk_kron = np.kron(Mk_kron, M2)
    assert np.array_equal(Mk_kron, mobius_mat(L))
print("TEST 1: M_{2^k} == M_2^{\\otimes k} (k-fold tensor power of the 2-bit [header, difference] split) for k=1..5: PASS")

# 2. Verify what happens when a gate f_0 in R_4 is stacked with its own sheet reading f_1 = sheet(f_0) = C(J(f_0))
#    (as in §204: Sum 0x69 = stack(XOR, sheet(XOR)) and Carry 0x17 = stack(AND, sheet(AND)))!
def J_int(x, L): return int(format(x, f'0{L}b')[::-1], 2)
def C_int(x, L): return x ^ ((1 << L) - 1)
def sheet_int(x, L): return C_int(J_int(x, L), L)

def word_to_vec(x, L):
    return np.array([(x >> (L - 1 - u)) & 1 for u in range(L)], dtype=int)
def vec_to_word(v):
    res = 0
    for b in v: res = (res << 1) | int(b)
    return res

M4 = mobius_mat(4)
M8 = mobius_mat(8)

print("\nTEST 2: Stacking every 2-input gate f_0 in R_4 with its De Morgan dual f_1 = sheet(f_0):")
for f0 in range(16):
    f1 = sheet_int(f0, 4)
    stacked = (f0 << 4) | f1
    anf_stacked = vec_to_word((M8 @ word_to_vec(stacked, 8)) % 2)
    anf_f0 = vec_to_word((M4 @ word_to_vec(f0, 4)) % 2)
    anf_diff = vec_to_word((M4 @ word_to_vec(f0 ^ f1, 4)) % 2)
    assert anf_stacked == ((anf_f0 << 4) | anf_diff)
    if f0 in (0b0001, 0b0110, 0b0111, 0b1101):
        print(f"  f0={f0:04b} (ANF={anf_f0:04b}), sheet(f0)={f1:04b} -> stack=0x{stacked:02X} -> ANF(stack)=0x{anf_stacked:02X} = [{anf_f0:04b} || {anf_diff:04b}]")
```
